GlobalProtect connection with PoC client fingerprint: Win10 Pro 64-bit + empty d AIExploitation Medium
Hunts post-PoC-release GlobalProtect gateway-connected events whose endpoint_os_version field equals 'Microsoft Windows 10 Pro 64-bit' AND whose source_user_info.domain field is empty — the hard-coded client fingerprint left in the public PoC for CVE-2026-0257. Domain-joined corporate endpoints almost never present an empty domain. Rationale: Targets the exact two hard-coded PoC client-configuration values Unit 42 published for post-2026-05-29 hunting: endpoint_os_version literal 'Microsoft Windows 10 Pro 64-bit' AND empty source_user_info.domain. Sentinel regex uses a verbatim @-string so backslashes do not collide with JSON/KQL string escaping (fix for prior validation error). Defender query is an environment baseline — local-only Win10 Pro hosts that would plausibly emit an empty domain. Cross-checked against: • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/active-exploitation-of-pan-os-cve-2026-0257/
ATT&CKT1078 T1556 T1133
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate BYOD / workgroup (non-domain-joined) Win10 Pro 64-bit users connecting to GlobalProtect with split-tunnel personal devices
  • Newly-imaged Win10 Pro laptop that has not yet been domain-joined when first GP connection is made
  • Contractor / consultant endpoints connecting from their own workgroup machines
  • QA / test rigs running stock Win10 Pro images without AD enrolment
  • Home-lab roaming users whose corporate device was rebuilt and lost domain trust
Suppression filter
| where SourceUserName !in (dynamic(["contractor-svc","byod-pilot-user"])) and SourceIP !in (dynamic(["<known-byod-egress-cidr>"]))
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: confirm the src_user is one that SHOULD only connect from a domain-joined device per corporate policy; check device-posture-check / HIP report for the same session — a legitimate corp endpoint would report domain membership. Step 2 — Contain: if user is on the domain-joined-only policy, force-logout the GP session, revoke the user's tokens, and require step-up MFA + device re-enrolment before the next login. Step 3 — Hunt: pivot on the tunnel-assigned internal IP for the next 24h across DeviceNetworkEvents and SecurityEvent 4624/4625; cross-correlate with the IP-IOC UC and the spoofed-host-id UC to find overlap; review whether other users from the same src_ip exhibit the same fingerprint.
DeviceInfo
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where OSPlatform =~ "Windows10" and OSVersion has "Pro"
| where isempty(LoggedOnUsers) or LoggedOnUsers !has "domain"
| summarize LastSeen = max(Timestamp) by DeviceId, DeviceName, OSPlatform, OSVersion, JoinType
| where JoinType !in~ ("AzureAD","Hybrid","Domain")
| project LastSeen, DeviceName, OSPlatform, OSVersion, JoinType
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where DeviceVendor =~ "Palo Alto Networks"
| where DeviceProduct has_any ("GlobalProtect", "PAN-OS")
| where Activity has_any ("gateway-connected", "GlobalProtect connected")
| where Message has "Microsoft Windows 10 Pro 64-bit"
| where Message matches regex @"(?i)source_user_info\.domain\s*[:=]\s*(?:,|;|$|\s)"
   or Message matches regex @"(?i)domain\s*[:=]\s*(?:,|;|$|\s)"
| project TimeGenerated, SourceIP, SourceUserName, SourceHostName, DestinationIP, Activity, Message
| order by TimeGenerated desc
source:pan_os (@evt.name:gateway-connected OR @evt.name:"GlobalProtect connected") @endpoint_os_version:"Microsoft Windows 10 Pro 64-bit" (-@source_user_info.domain:* OR @source_user_info.domain:"")
APPLICATION
`pan_globalprotect` (event_id="gateway-connected" OR event_id="GlobalProtect connected" OR log_subtype="gateway-connect")
    endpoint_os_version="Microsoft Windows 10 Pro 64-bit"
    (source_user_info.domain="" OR NOT source_user_info.domain=*)

| convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
| where count >= 1
Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc
NETWORK
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
| tstats summariesonly=false count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-0257")

| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-0257")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

Aikido
2026-06-09 11 use cases 5 techniques 6 kill-chain phases detected

Deep dive into binding.gyp, the often overlooked npm build file that can execute malicious code at install time through shell expansions, sandbox escapes, and compiler hijacking. Category: Vulnerabilities & Threats

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Miasma-tainted package install: binding.gyp dropped into known-compromised npm package paths
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Wait, binding.gyp Can Do What? Exploring npm's Weirdest Build System
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Miasma Phantom Gyp: python.exe (gyp parser) spawning node index.js during npm install · [LLM] Miasma payload SHA256 hash hit (published Phantom Gyp IOCs) · Suspicious browser extension installation · File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Infostealer — non-browser process accessing browser cookie/login DBs · Crypto-wallet file/keystore access by non-wallet process
Miasma Phantom Gyp: python.exe (gyp parser) spawning node index.js during npm install AIInstallation High
Detects the core Miasma v2 abuse where node-gyp's Python parser executes a GYP command-expansion (<!(...)>) embedded in binding.gyp, spawning the credential-stealer payload as a child of python.exe rather than via package.json lifecycle hooks. Hunts the specific parent→child shape the worm produces during dependency install. Rationale: Anchored on the exact process tree the article describes: node-gyp invokes Python (gyp.py) to parse binding.gyp, and the embedded <!(node index.js > /dev/null 2>&1 && echo stub.c) command spawns node.exe with index.js as a direct child of python.exe — a parent→child shape that legitimate native add-on builds never produce (those go python→cl.exe/link.exe/cmake.exe). Allow-list of real toolchain children suppresses normal node-gyp activity. Cross-checked Semgrep, Endor Labs, StepSecurity write-ups confirming the python→node payload chain. Cross-checked against: • https://semgrep.dev/blog/2026/miasma-v2-self-spreading-npm-worm-now-uses-malicious-bindinggyp-file-and-compromises-57-packages/ • https://www.endorlabs.com/learn/malicious-payload-in-ai-sdk-ollama-npm-package • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/binding-gyp-npm-supply-chain-attack-spreads-like-worm • https://corgea.com/research/miasma-phantom-gyp-npm-worm-vapi-ai-sdk-ollama-june-2026
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059.006 T1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate packages (e.g. node-pre-gyp, node-sass forks) where binding.gyp uses <!(node -e '...') to probe Node/Python versions at configure time
  • Developers running 'node-gyp rebuild --verbose' manually during native-addon debugging on a developer workstation
  • CI agents building Electron / better-sqlite3 / canvas / sharp where the configure step invokes node helpers (rare but possible)
  • Internal tooling that wraps node-gyp with custom Python pre-processors for cross-compilation
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("node-pre-gyp","--target_platform","--target_arch") and ProcessCommandLine has "-e" and ProcessCommandLine has "process.version")
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: pull the full process tree (npm.cmd → node.exe → python.exe → matching child). Inspect the binding.gyp at the parent's working directory (look for <!(...), <!@(...), or pymod_do_main tokens); review the child's command line for 'index.js' or sandbox-escape Python literals. Confirm which npm package owns the node_modules subdirectory the binding.gyp came from. Step 2 — Contain: isolate the host from network, revoke any npm/GitHub/AWS/GCP/Azure/Vault tokens that resided on it (Miasma harvests all of these), force-rotate the developer's npm 2FA recovery codes, and pause CI runners using the same npm cache. Step 3 — Hunt: pivot on InitiatingProcessAccountObjectId across DeviceFileEvents for reads of ~/.aws/credentials, ~/.ssh/id_rsa, ~/.npmrc, ~/.docker/config.json, ~/.config/gh/hosts.yml in the same window; check DeviceNetworkEvents for outbound to api.github.com / *.workers.dev from that node.exe PID; review npm audit log and GitHub audit log for any package republicatio
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("python.exe","python3.exe","python","python3")
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("gyp.py","node-gyp","binding.gyp","gyp_main")
| where FileName !in~ ("cl.exe","link.exe","cmake.exe","msbuild.exe","gcc.exe","g++.exe","clang.exe","clang++.exe","make.exe","nmake.exe","ld.exe","tracker.exe","rc.exe","cvtres.exe","mt.exe","conhost.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("node.exe","node","cmd.exe","sh","bash","dash","curl.exe","wget.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe")
   or ProcessCommandLine has_any ("index.js","__subclasses__","catch_warnings","echo stub.c","/dev/null 2>&1","__import__('os')","pymod_do_main")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFolderPath has_any ("node_modules","npm-cache","_npx","yarn","pnpm") or InitiatingProcessParentFileName in~ ("node.exe","npm.cmd","npx.cmd","yarn.cmd","pnpm.cmd")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessParentFileName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate

| where not(ActingProcessName matches regex @"(?i)[\\/](cl|link|cmake|msbuild|gcc|g\+\+|clang|clang\+\+|make|nmake|ld|tracker|rc|cvtres|mt|conhost)\.exe$")
| where ActingProcessName matches regex @"(?i)[\\/](node|cmd|sh|bash|dash|curl|wget|powershell|pwsh)(\.exe)?$"
   or ActingProcessCommandLine has_any ("index.js","__subclasses__","catch_warnings","echo stub.c","/dev/null 2>&1","__import__('os')","pymod_do_main")
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"

title: Miasma Phantom Gyp - Python gyp parser spawning Node payload via binding.gyp
id: 7f3b6c2a-d4e1-4a01-9c5e-3a4f1c8e7b22
description: Detects node-gyp's Python parser (gyp.py) spawning unexpected child processes during npm install, indicative of GYP command-expansion abuse used by the Miasma v2 / Phantom Gyp worm (June 2026)
references:
  - https://www.aikido.dev/blog/exploring-binding-gyp-npm-build-system
  - https://semgrep.dev/blog/2026/miasma-v2-self-spreading-npm-worm-now-uses-malicious-bindinggyp-file-and-compromises-57-packages/
  - https://www.endorlabs.com/learn/malicious-payload-in-ai-sdk-ollama-npm-package
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/06/09
tags:
  - attack.initial_access
  - attack.t1195.002
  - attack.execution
  - attack.t1059.006
  - attack.t1059.007
logsource:
  category: process_creation
  product: windows
detection:
  parent_python_gyp:
    ParentImage|endswith:
      - '\python.exe'
      - '\python3.exe'
    ParentCommandLine|contains:
      - 'gyp.py'
      - 'node-gyp'
      - 'binding.gyp'
  suspicious_child_image:
    Image|endswith:
      - '\node.exe'
      - '\cmd.exe'
      - '\sh.exe'
      - '\bash.exe'
      - '\curl.exe'
      - '\wget.exe'
      - '\powershell.exe'
      - '\pwsh.exe'
  suspicious_child_cmdline:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - 'index.js'
      - '__subclasses__'
      - 'catch_warnings'
      - 'echo stub.c'
      - "__import__('os')"
      - 'pymod_do_main'
  filter_compilers:
    Image|endswith:
      - '\cl.exe'
      - '\link.exe'
      - '\cmake.exe'
      - '\msbuild.exe'
      - '\gcc.exe'
      - '\clang.exe'
      - '\make.exe'
      - '\nmake.exe'
  condition: parent_python_gyp and (suspicious_child_image or suspicious_child_cmdline) and not filter_compilers
falsepositives:
  - Legitimate packages whose binding.gyp uses <!(node ...) for non-malicious build-time configuration (e.g. node-pre-gyp version probing)
  - Developers manually invoking node-gyp rebuild for native add-on debugging
level: high
source:(windows.sysmon OR windows.security) @EventID:(1 OR 4688) (@ParentImage:(*\\python.exe OR *\\Python.exe OR *\\PYTHON.EXE OR *\\python3.exe) OR @ParentCommandLine:(*gyp.py* OR *node-gyp* OR *binding.gyp*)) AND (@Image:(*\\node.exe OR *\\Node.exe OR *\\NODE.EXE OR *\\cmd.exe OR *\\CMD.EXE OR *\\powershell.exe OR *\\pwsh.exe OR *\\curl.exe OR *\\wget.exe) OR @CommandLine:(*index.js* OR *__subclasses__* OR *catch_warnings* OR *"echo stub.c"* OR *pymod_do_main* OR *__import__* )) AND -@Image:(*\\cl.exe OR *\\link.exe OR *\\cmake.exe OR *\\msbuild.exe OR *\\gcc.exe OR *\\clang.exe OR *\\make.exe OR *\\nmake.exe)

Miasma-tainted package install: binding.gyp dropped into known-compromised npm package paths AIDelivery High
File-event detection for binding.gyp files written into node_modules subdirectories of the publicly named Miasma v2 victims (@vapi-ai/server-sdk, ai-sdk-ollama, @redhat-cloud-services/*). These packages historically had no native add-on, so the mere presence of binding.gyp inside their node_modules path is the campaign signature. Rationale: The article explicitly names @vapi-ai/server-sdk (71k weekly downloads) and ai-sdk-ollama (31k weekly downloads) as victims and references the @redhat-cloud-services namespace; corroborating sources confirm these packages historically had no native code, so binding.gyp inside their tree is the campaign signature. Detection is path-anchored to those exact package names rather than generic binding.gyp activity. Cross-checked Semgrep, Chainguard, and Endor Labs. Cross-checked against: • https://semgrep.dev/blog/2026/miasma-v2-self-spreading-npm-worm-now-uses-malicious-bindinggyp-file-and-compromises-57-packages/ • https://www.chainguard.dev/unchained/chainguard-artifacts-safe-from-miasma-phantom-gyp-npm-attack • https://www.endorlabs.com/learn/malicious-payload-in-ai-sdk-ollama-npm-package
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1505.005
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A vendor-published clean (post-incident) version of one of these packages ships a legitimate binding.gyp — verify file hash against the npm registry tarball
  • Local file restore from backup containing an old node_modules tree
  • Forensic copy / IR engineer staging the malicious sample onto a sandbox host for analysis
  • A developer cloning the repo (not via npm) where a legitimate maintainer added a build-time helper in binding.gyp
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: read the dropped binding.gyp content (extract from disk or EDR file-capture) and confirm it contains <!(...), <!@(...), pymod_do_main, or Python sandbox-escape literals (__subclasses__, catch_warnings). Confirm the installed package version matches the IOC range (@vapi-ai/server-sdk >0.11.0, ai-sdk-ollama >0.13.0). Step 2 — Contain: stop any in-flight node-gyp rebuild PID, isolate the host, and treat ALL credentials reachable from the host as burned — npm tokens, GitHub PATs, AWS/GCP/Azure CLI configs, kubeconfigs, Vault tokens, .npmrc auth. Trigger automated rotation. Step 3 — Hunt: query all hosts and CI runners for the same package-path pattern in the last 14 days; check the maintainer's npm account for unauthorized package republications via npm audit logs; review GitHub org for newly created repos under the developer's account (Miasma uses GitHub for dead-drop exfil).
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where FileName =~ "binding.gyp"
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")
| where FolderPath has_any (
    @"node_modules\@vapi-ai\server-sdk",
    "node_modules/@vapi-ai/server-sdk",
    @"node_modules\ai-sdk-ollama",
    "node_modules/ai-sdk-ollama",
    @"node_modules\@redhat-cloud-services",
    "node_modules/@redhat-cloud-services")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("git.exe","code.exe","explorer.exe")

Miasma payload SHA256 hash hit (published Phantom Gyp IOCs) AIInstallation Medium
Hash-based match for the four SHA256s associated with the Miasma v2 Phantom Gyp payload set. Fires on either file write or process execution telemetry. Universal IOC sweep across endpoint estate. Rationale: Direct hash IOC sweep against the four SHA256s the article ships. Hash detections are non-negotiable for any campaign with published IOCs — universal, low-FP, and works across file-event and process-event surfaces. Coverage spans both DeviceFileEvents (catches the dropped artifact even if it never executes) and DeviceProcessEvents (catches execution attempts). Cross-checked against: • https://semgrep.dev/blog/2026/miasma-v2-self-spreading-npm-worm-now-uses-malicious-bindinggyp-file-and-compromises-57-packages/
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Forensic analyst or malware researcher intentionally placing the IOC sample on a sandbox / detonation host
  • Security tooling (e.g. EDR test harness, YARA-rule unit test fixtures) staging the hash for validation
  • Threat-intel platform pre-caching the file for hash-lookup tests
  • Inbound email or chat attachment carrying the sample for IR analysis (will hit Defender mail scan first)
Suppression filter
| where DeviceName !in ("SOC-SANDBOX-01","IR-WORKSTATION-01","MALWARE-LAB-01")
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: confirm the host is not a sanctioned forensic / sandbox / IR-analyst workstation by checking the device tag and the user's role in IdentityInfo. Pull the full file path and parent process for the hash hit; if it executed (DeviceProcessEvents row), the host is presumed compromised. Step 2 — Contain: isolate the device immediately via Live Response / Defender machine action, suspend the user's npm / GitHub / cloud sessions, kill the offending PID. Treat all secrets reachable from the host as burned and force rotation (npm tokens, GitHub PATs, AWS keys, .npmrc auth, kubeconfig, Vault tokens). Step 3 — Hunt: pivot on InitiatingProcessAccountObjectId and SHA256 across the full estate for the last 30 days; check whether any package on npm under the user's maintainer account was published in the same window (Miasma republishes from victim hosts); review GitHub audit log for new repos under the user's account in the last 7 days (potential dead-drop exfil endpoint).
let MiasmaHashes = dynamic([
  "ef641e956f91d501b748085996303c96a64d67f63bfeef0dda175e5aa19cca90",
  "288f26c2eadcb1a7923fe376d16f5404216cce15d9fc162a4a78574dc7df399a",
  "e3dbe63aded45278f49c4746ab938ed9472b36def79b43e2dd2d7eff014481d1",
  "ceff7c51d70832c3ec8dd2744b606a23b3c924ef664ae23439b9b742ea154108"
]);
union
  ( DeviceFileEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
    | where SHA256 in (MiasmaHashes)
    | project Timestamp, Source="FileEvent", DeviceName, AccountName=InitiatingProcessAccountName, Path=FolderPath, FileName, SHA256, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine ),
  ( DeviceProcessEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
    | where SHA256 in (MiasmaHashes)
    | project Timestamp, Source="ProcessEvent", DeviceName, AccountName, Path=FolderPath, FileName, SHA256, InitiatingProcessFileName=InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine=ProcessCommandLine )
| order by Timestamp desc

Node child of node-gyp/python making outbound to GitHub dead-drop or anomalous web service during install AIexfil Medium
Catches the Miasma exfiltration step: after the binding.gyp command-expansion fires, the spawned node.exe (running index.js) beacons cloud credentials and CI tokens out to attacker-controlled GitHub repositories or webhook services. Pivots on the rare combination of node.exe with a node-gyp/python ancestor making outbound TCP to api.github.com, raw.githubusercontent.com, or first-seen external hosts. Rationale: Anchors on the article's described post-execution behavior (credential exfil to attacker-controlled dead-drops) with the parent-process chain unique to binding.gyp abuse. Corroborating sources (Endor Labs, Morphisec, OX Security) confirm Miasma exfils to GitHub dead-drop repos and uses Workers/webhook bouncers; this detection requires both the gyp ancestry AND the suspicious destination to fire, keeping FPs manageable while catching the campaign-specific exfil shape. Cross-checked against: • https://www.endorlabs.com/learn/malicious-payload-in-ai-sdk-ollama-npm-package • https://www.morphisec.com/blog/its-in-your-ai-assistant-now-shai-hulud-wave-3-and-the-miasma-worm-targeting-npm/ • https://www.ox.security/blog/600000-monthly-downloads-affected-miasma-supply-chain-attack-is-back-on-npm/
ATT&CKT1567.001 T1552.001 T1041
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate npm packages whose postinstall hook fetches assets from raw.githubusercontent.com (e.g. node-pre-gyp downloading prebuilt binaries) — but those typically run as direct child of npm, not via python.exe (gyp) chain
  • node-gyp packages that pull source tarballs from GitHub during configure step (rare; node-pre-gyp uses HTTP downloader)
  • CI runners that genuinely push to api.github.com via node-based GitHub API client called from a build hook
  • Cloudflare Workers used by an org's internal build accelerator service
Suppression filter
| where not (ParentProcessCommandLine has_any ("node-pre-gyp","prebuild-install") and DstHostname has "objects.githubusercontent.com")
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: pull the spawning binding.gyp content and the node process's full argv; identify which npm package owns the source path. Check whether the GitHub destination repo is org-controlled (legitimate) or an externally owned account (likely dead-drop). Step 2 — Contain: kill the node.exe PID, isolate the device, and immediately rotate every credential reachable from the host — focus first on the developer's GitHub PAT (worm publishes via it), npm publish token, AWS keys in ~/.aws/credentials, .npmrc auth, kubeconfig, Vault tokens, GCP/Azure CLI cache. Step 3 — Hunt: query GitHub audit log for new repository creations under the developer's user account in the last 24h (Miasma creates ephemeral dead-drop repos); search ImNetworkSession for any other node.exe egress to the same destination from CI runners in the last 7 days; check npm registry for unauthorized package publications under the dev's maintainer accounts.
let gyp_node_children = DeviceProcessEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
    | where FileName =~ "node.exe"
    | where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("python.exe","python3.exe","cmd.exe","sh","bash")
    | where InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("gyp.py","binding.gyp","node-gyp")
        or InitiatingProcessParentFileName in~ ("python.exe","python3.exe")
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | project ProcSpawnTime=Timestamp, DeviceId, DeviceName, AccountName, ProcessId, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessCommandLine;
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "node.exe"
| where RemoteUrl has_any ("api.github.com","raw.githubusercontent.com","objects.githubusercontent.com",".workers.dev","webhook.site","ngrok.io","hooks.slack.com","discord.com/api/webhooks","transfer.sh","file.io","0x0.st")
   or RemoteIPType == "Public"
| join kind=inner gyp_node_children on DeviceId, $left.InitiatingProcessId == $right.ProcessId
| where Timestamp between (ProcSpawnTime .. ProcSpawnTime + 5m)
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort, ProcessCommandLine=InitiatingProcessCommandLine, GypParentCmd=InitiatingProcessCommandLine1
| order by Timestamp desc
let gyp_node_children = ImProcessCreate
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
    | where ActingProcessName matches regex @"(?i)[\\/]node(\.exe)?$"
    | where ParentProcessName matches regex @"(?i)[\\/](python3?|cmd|sh|bash)(\.exe)?$"
    | where ParentProcessCommandLine has_any ("gyp.py","binding.gyp","node-gyp")
    | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
    | project ProcSpawnTime=TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActingProcessId, ParentProcessCommandLine;
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where DstHostname has_any ("api.github.com","raw.githubusercontent.com","objects.githubusercontent.com",".workers.dev","webhook.site","ngrok.io","hooks.slack.com","discord.com","transfer.sh","file.io","0x0.st")
| join kind=inner gyp_node_children on DvcHostname
| where TimeGenerated between (ProcSpawnTime .. ProcSpawnTime + 5m)
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, SrcUsername, DstHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber, NetworkBytes, ParentProcessCommandLine

PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command Exploitation High
Encoded or obfuscated PowerShell — common across loaders, recon, and post-exploitation.
ATT&CKT1059.001 T1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe")
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(-enc|encodedcommand|frombase64string|-nop|-w\s+hidden|invoke-expression|iex\s*\(|downloadstring|net\.webclient)"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\pwsh.exe"
| where CommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(-enc|encodedcommand|frombase64string|-nop|-w\s+hidden|invoke-expression|iex\s*\(|downloadstring|net\.webclient)"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName,
          CommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

status: experimental
description: |

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("ef641e956f91d501b748085996303c96a64d67f63bfeef0dda175e5aa19cca90", "288f26c2eadcb1a7923fe376d16f5404216cce15d9fc162a4a78574dc7df399a", "e3dbe63aded45278f49c4746ab938ed9472b36def79b43e2dd2d7eff014481d1", "ceff7c51d70832c3ec8dd2744b606a23b3c924ef664ae23439b9b742ea154108") or SHA1 in~ ("ef641e956f91d501b748085996303c96a64d67f63bfeef0dda175e5aa19cca90", "288f26c2eadcb1a7923fe376d16f5404216cce15d9fc162a4a78574dc7df399a", "e3dbe63aded45278f49c4746ab938ed9472b36def79b43e2dd2d7eff014481d1", "ceff7c51d70832c3ec8dd2744b606a23b3c924ef664ae23439b9b742ea154108") or MD5 in~ ("ef641e956f91d501b748085996303c96a64d67f63bfeef0dda175e5aa19cca90", "288f26c2eadcb1a7923fe376d16f5404216cce15d9fc162a4a78574dc7df399a", "e3dbe63aded45278f49c4746ab938ed9472b36def79b43e2dd2d7eff014481d1", "ceff7c51d70832c3ec8dd2744b606a23b3c924ef664ae23439b9b742ea154108")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("ef641e956f91d501b748085996303c96a64d67f63bfeef0dda175e5aa19cca90", "288f26c2eadcb1a7923fe376d16f5404216cce15d9fc162a4a78574dc7df399a", "e3dbe63aded45278f49c4746ab938ed9472b36def79b43e2dd2d7eff014481d1", "ceff7c51d70832c3ec8dd2744b606a23b3c924ef664ae23439b9b742ea154108") or SHA1 in~ ("ef641e956f91d501b748085996303c96a64d67f63bfeef0dda175e5aa19cca90", "288f26c2eadcb1a7923fe376d16f5404216cce15d9fc162a4a78574dc7df399a", "e3dbe63aded45278f49c4746ab938ed9472b36def79b43e2dd2d7eff014481d1", "ceff7c51d70832c3ec8dd2744b606a23b3c924ef664ae23439b9b742ea154108") or MD5 in~ ("ef641e956f91d501b748085996303c96a64d67f63bfeef0dda175e5aa19cca90", "288f26c2eadcb1a7923fe376d16f5404216cce15d9fc162a4a78574dc7df399a", "e3dbe63aded45278f49c4746ab938ed9472b36def79b43e2dd2d7eff014481d1", "ceff7c51d70832c3ec8dd2744b606a23b3c924ef664ae23439b9b742ea154108")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["ef641e956f91d501b748085996303c96a64d67f63bfeef0dda175e5aa19cca90", "288f26c2eadcb1a7923fe376d16f5404216cce15d9fc162a4a78574dc7df399a", "e3dbe63aded45278f49c4746ab938ed9472b36def79b43e2dd2d7eff014481d1", "ceff7c51d70832c3ec8dd2744b606a23b3c924ef664ae23439b9b742ea154108"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

[GHSA / CRITICAL] CVE-2026-47724: nebula-mesh: API endpoints lack ownership checks, enabling cross-operator privilege escalation

GitHub Security Advisories
2026-06-08 6 use cases 1 technique 4 kill-chain phases detected

nebula-mesh: API endpoints lack ownership checks, enabling cross-operator privilege escalation

CVEsCVE-2026-47724

nebula-mesh CVE-2026-47724 — cross-operator admin API key mint via POST /api/v1/operators/{id}/api-keys AIExploitation High
Hunts the exact endpoint abused in exploit chain A — handleCreateOperatorAPIKey lacks any admin/ownership check pre-v0.3.4, so any successful POST to /api/v1/operators/{id}/api-keys by a non-admin actor mints a fresh bearer for an arbitrary operator (including admins). On an unpatched server this single request is privilege escalation. Rationale: Endpoint path /api/v1/operators/{id}/api-keys and the handleCreateOperatorAPIKey function at internal/api/operators.go:118 are quoted verbatim from the GHSA — exploit chain A delivers an admin bearer in one curl with no ownership check pre-v0.3.4. Successful POST on this exact path is the signal. No external corroboration available — CVE freshly minted (2026-47724) and GHSA is the authoritative source.
ATT&CKT1068 T1078.003 T1190
Data sourcesWeb
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate admin provisioning API keys for a newly created operator via the admin UI
  • Automated key rotation cron job owned by the ops team hitting this endpoint on a schedule
  • IaC/Terraform provider running operator-bootstrap during deployment
  • First-time setup script that seeds the initial admin operator and its API keys
  • Internal API health check or integration test that creates and immediately deletes a key
Suppression filter
// Sentinel example — suppress known admin sources and automation user-agents
| where cIP !in ("10.10.0.5", "10.10.0.6")  // ops bastion IPs
| where csUserAgent !startswith "terraform-provider-nebula/"
| where csUserAgent !contains "nebula-keyrotate-cron"
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the bearer token's owning operator_id from the nebula-mesh app audit log for this request; if actor operator_id != target operator_id in the URL path, or actor is non-admin and target is an admin operator, treat as confirmed exploit. Capture the returned API key ID from the response body for forensics. 2) Contain: immediately revoke the newly minted bearer via DB-level UPDATE on operator_api_keys (do not use the API — attacker may still hold admin); rotate the source operator's key; force-upgrade the server to v0.3.4+. 3) Hunt: replay the last 30 days of /api/v1/operators/*/api-keys POSTs from the same source IP and bearer; pivot into /api/v1/hosts/*/reenroll, /api/v1/enroll, and /api/v1/networks/*/firewall calls within the same session window to scope blast radius.
// Primary: W3CIISLog when nebula-mesh is fronted by IIS/Azure App Gateway. Adapt cs* fields for AppServiceHTTPLogs or CommonSecurityLog (RequestURL/RequestMethod) as needed.
W3CIISLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where csMethod == "POST"
| where csUriStem matches regex @"^/api/v1/operators/[^/]+/api-keys/?$"
| where scStatus in (200, 201)
| extend TargetOperatorId = extract(@"/api/v1/operators/([^/]+)/api-keys", 1, csUriStem)
| extend CVE = "CVE-2026-47724"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, ClientIP=cIP, AuthUser=csUserName, csMethod, csUriStem, TargetOperatorId, scStatus, UserAgent=csUserAgent, CVE
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: nebula-mesh CVE-2026-47724 Operator API Key Creation
id: 8c5b4f12-9a2d-4e3c-b6a1-7f3e1d8b9c01
status: experimental
description: Detects successful POST to nebula-mesh /api/v1/operators/{id}/api-keys, which on versions < 0.3.4 lacks admin/ownership checks (CVE-2026-47724) and lets any operator key mint a fresh bearer for any operator including admins.
references:

author: Detection Engineering
date: 2026/06/09

nebula-mesh CVE-2026-47724 — cross-tenant host identity hijack via /hosts/{id}/reenroll → /enroll chain AIInstallation High
Detects exploit chain B: a single source POSTs to /api/v1/hosts/{id}/reenroll (mints a single-use enrollment token for an arbitrary host with no ownership check) then immediately POSTs to /api/v1/enroll with attacker-controlled X25519/Ed25519 keys, overwriting signing_pub_pem and the cert. Same-source temporal correlation within ~60s on these two specific paths is the textbook exploitation signature. Rationale: Both endpoints (/api/v1/hosts/{id}/reenroll and /api/v1/enroll) and the temporal chain are explicitly walked in the article (internal/api/hosts.go:321,330 → mintEnrollmentTokenForHost, then enroll.go:175 overwrites signing_pub_pem). The 60-second window is generous — exploit code typically chains these in <5s. Sigma skipped because this is multi-event temporal correlation that single-event Sigma can't express.
ATT&CKT1098.001 T1212 T1552.004 T1550.001
Data sourcesWeb
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate operator-initiated identity rotation for a host whose key was suspected compromised
  • Automated post-incident reenrollment workflow rotating multiple hosts in sequence
  • Lab/staging environment provisioning script that reenrolls test hosts at deploy time
  • Agent recovery flow when a host's local key store is corrupted and ops triggers reenroll
  • Bulk migration tooling moving hosts between CAs (legitimately authorised admin action)
Suppression filter
// Sentinel — exclude known ops-bastion IPs and the legit reenrollment automation
| where cIP !in ("10.10.0.5", "10.10.0.6")
| where ReenrollAgent !startswith "nebula-rotate-cli/"
Response runbook
1) Triage: from the nebula-mesh DB, compare the bearer token's owning operator_id (actor) to the CAID of the host referenced in /hosts/{victim_host_id}/reenroll — if actor's operator does not own the host's CA, this is the exploit. Pull the signing_pub_pem and cert before/after rows from the hosts audit table. 2) Contain: invalidate the new cert on the affected host(s), force a fresh CA-signed reenroll from an admin operator, suspend the source bearer token, and force-upgrade to v0.3.4+. Watch for incoming 'bad_signature' poll failures from the legitimate agent — that confirms the identity was overwritten. 3) Hunt: for the same source IP and bearer, list every /hosts/*/reenroll and /enroll call across the lookback window to enumerate every host whose identity may have been seized; also pivot into /networks/*/firewall and /hosts/*/mobile-bundle activity from the same session.
let Lookback = 7d;
let WindowSec = 60;
let Reenroll = W3CIISLog
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(Lookback)
    | where csMethod == "POST"
    | where csUriStem matches regex @"^/api/v1/hosts/[^/]+/reenroll/?$"
    | where scStatus in (200, 201)
    | extend VictimHostId = extract(@"/api/v1/hosts/([^/]+)/reenroll", 1, csUriStem)
    | project ReenrollTime = TimeGenerated, Computer, cIP, csUserName, VictimHostId, ReenrollAgent = csUserAgent;
let Enroll = W3CIISLog
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(Lookback)
    | where csMethod == "POST"
    | where csUriStem matches regex @"^/api/v1/enroll/?$"
    | where scStatus in (200, 201)
    | project EnrollTime = TimeGenerated, Computer, cIP, EnrollAgent = csUserAgent;
Reenroll
| join kind=inner Enroll on cIP, Computer
| where EnrollTime between (ReenrollTime .. ReenrollTime + WindowSec * 1s)
| extend DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', EnrollTime, ReenrollTime)
| extend CVE = "CVE-2026-47724"
| project ReenrollTime, EnrollTime, DelaySec, Computer, cIP, csUserName, VictimHostId, ReenrollAgent, EnrollAgent, CVE
| order by ReenrollTime desc

nebula-mesh CVE-2026-47724 — operator roster + API key metadata enumeration burst AIReconnaissance Medium
Hunts the discovery phase of exploit chain A — a single source/bearer fanning out across GET /api/v1/operators and GET /api/v1/operators/{id}/api-keys (handleListOperators and handleListOperatorAPIKeys both lack admin gates per the GHSA) to identify admin operators and their key IDs before minting the privileged bearer. Rationale: Article enumerates the five operator-management handlers missing admin gates (operators.go:66 list, :79 disable, :94 enable, :157 revoke, :173 list-keys). Combining GET /api/v1/operators with multiple GET /api/v1/operators/{id}/api-keys in a short window is the natural recon shape before exploit A. Sigma skipped — threshold-based aggregation isn't expressible in single-event Sigma.
ATT&CKT1087 T1526 T1069.003
Data sourcesWeb
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Admin opening the operator-management dashboard, which fans out one list + per-operator key calls
  • Periodic operator inventory script run by ops to backup key metadata
  • SOC auditor performing a weekly access review across all operators
  • Monitoring/observability tooling polling these endpoints for an operator-health dashboard
  • Onboarding script for a new ops engineer exploring the API surface
Suppression filter
| where csUserAgent !startswith "nebula-admin-ui/"
| where csUserAgent !contains "nebula-audit-export"
Response runbook
1) Triage: in the nebula-mesh app audit log identify the bearer behind these GETs; if the actor's operator_id is non-admin AND the listed operator IDs include any admin operator, escalate. Inspect the response bodies (in the audit log) to confirm key metadata was returned. 2) Contain: if confirmed recon, rotate the suspect operator's bearer token immediately and tighten WAF/API-gateway rules to require admin auth on /api/v1/operators* before the v0.3.4 upgrade lands. 3) Hunt: from the same actor and source IP, pivot forward in time to any POST /api/v1/operators/*/api-keys (UC1), POST /api/v1/hosts/*/reenroll (UC2), or PUT /api/v1/networks/*/firewall (UC4) — recon almost always immediately precedes one of these.

nebula-mesh CVE-2026-47724 — cross-tenant firewall mutation via PUT /api/v1/networks/{id}/firewall AIActions on Objectives Medium
Detects state-changing writes to nebula-mesh firewall configuration on the /api/v1/networks/{id}/firewall endpoint, which trusts bearer auth alone pre-v0.3.4 per exploit chain C. An attacker with cross-tenant API access can open or remove firewall rules in any network in the deployment. Rationale: Article calls out /api/v1/networks/{id}/firewall (get, PUT) as part of exploit chain C and the CVSS rationale flags 'Mass cross-tenant CRUD including firewall-rule mutation' as a primary impact. Writes (PUT/POST) are the high-fidelity slice; reads would be too noisy.
ATT&CKT1562.007 T1565.001 T1098
Data sourcesWeb
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Operator updating firewall rules in a network they own via the admin UI
  • IaC/Terraform pipeline rolling out a routine network policy change
  • Scheduled rule reconciliation script flattening drift back to declared state
  • Incident-response playbook tightening rules in response to an unrelated alert
  • Migration tooling copying rules from a staging network to production
Suppression filter
| where csUserAgent !startswith "terraform-provider-nebula/"
| where csUserAgent !contains "nebula-policy-recon"
Response runbook
1) Triage: query the nebula-mesh app audit log for the actor's operator_id and the network's CAID — if actor's operator does not own the network's CA, treat as exploit chain C. Diff the firewall_rules table at request_time-1s vs request_time+1s to enumerate the exact rule changes. 2) Contain: revert the firewall_rules table from the audit log's pre-change snapshot, then suspend the source bearer and force-upgrade to v0.3.4+. If overly permissive rules were inserted (e.g. allow-any), block at the upstream network layer immediately. 3) Hunt: from the same actor and IP, pivot to GET /api/v1/networks/* for reconnaissance preceding the write, and to /hosts/*/reenroll (UC2) and /operators/*/api-keys (UC1) for the rest of the kill chain.
W3CIISLog

| where csUriStem matches regex @"^/api/v1/networks/[^/]+/firewall/?$"

| extend NetworkId = extract(@"/api/v1/networks/([^/]+)/firewall", 1, csUriStem)
| extend CVE = "CVE-2026-47724"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, ClientIP=cIP, AuthUser=csUserName, csMethod, csUriStem, NetworkId, scStatus, UserAgent=csUserAgent, CVE
| order by TimeGenerated desc

nebula-mesh CVE-2026-47724 — operator sabotage (disable/enable/key revocation) by non-admin actor AIActions on Objectives High
Hunts the impact phase of CVE-2026-47724 — handleDisableOperator (line 79), handleEnableOperator (line 94), and handleRevokeOperatorAPIKey (line 157) all lack admin gates per the GHSA. An exploited bearer can disable peer operators, re-enable disabled ones, or revoke any operator's API keys to lock legitimate admins out. Rationale: Article's 'Affected operator-management handlers' section explicitly lists each of these three handlers and their line numbers as missing admin gates. Disable/enable/revoke against a non-self target operator from a non-admin actor is unambiguous exploitation. Falcon, Defender, and CloudWatch all left empty: these are HTTP API calls to a custom Go server, not endpoint/AWS telemetry — only web-tier logs see them.
ATT&CKT1531 T1098 T1496
Data sourcesWeb
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Admin legitimately disabling an operator as part of offboarding a contractor
  • Admin re-enabling an operator after a paused review
  • Scheduled credential cleanup script revoking expired API keys
  • Break-glass rotation of an operator's keys after a perceived compromise
  • Bulk migration script consolidating operator accounts pre-v0.3.4 upgrade
Suppression filter
| where csUserAgent !startswith "nebula-offboard/"
| where csUserAgent !contains "nebula-keyrotate-cron"
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the actor bearer's operator_id and admin flag from the nebula-mesh audit log — if actor is non-admin OR actor's operator_id != target operator_id, treat as confirmed sabotage. List every disabled operator and every revoked api_keys row touched by this actor in the lookback. 2) Contain: re-enable the affected peer operators directly via DB (bypass the API), regenerate revoked keys out-of-band, suspend the offending bearer, and force-upgrade to v0.3.4+ before re-opening the API. Notify affected operators via an out-of-band channel — they may be locked out of the API and need to be told. 3) Hunt: correlate forward into operator inventory (UC3), API key minting (UC1), and host reenroll chain (UC2) from the same actor — sabotage is usually the final stage; the recon and priv-esc happened earlier.

     or (csMethod == "DELETE" and csUriStem matches regex @"^/api/v1/operators/[^/]+/api-keys/[^/]+/?$")
| extend TargetOperatorId = extract(@"/api/v1/operators/([^/]+)/", 1, csUriStem)
| extend Action = case(
    csUriStem endswith "/disable", "operator_disable",
    csUriStem endswith "/enable",  "operator_enable",
    csUriStem contains "/api-keys/", "api_key_revoke",
    "other")
| extend CVE = "CVE-2026-47724"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, ClientIP=cIP, AuthUser=csUserName, csMethod, Action, TargetOperatorId, csUriStem, scStatus, UserAgent=csUserAgent, CVE
| order by TimeGenerated desc

osascript invoked with AppleScript breakout pattern (mismatched tell blocks + do shell script) AIExploitation High
Detects exploitation of CVE-2026-47252 in Anyquery: a SQL-controlled URL is interpolated unescaped into an AppleScript template and reaches `osascript -e`. The injected payload closes the original `tell application` block early, runs `do shell script`, then reopens a new `tell application` block — producing a command line containing both `end tell` and `do shell script` alongside two `tell application` openers. Rationale: Pulled the exact exploitation primitive from the PoC at tabs.go:141: the injected URL contains literal newlines that close one `tell application "Google Chrome"` block with `end tell`, runs `do shell script "id"`, then reopens a second `tell application` block. Detecting the co-occurrence of these three AppleScript tokens on an osascript command line — especially when spawned by anyquery — is a fingerprint of this CVE's exploitation. Cross-checked the GitHub advisory and the affected package paths (julien040/anyquery plugins/chrome, plugins/brave, plugins/edge, plugins/safari). Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-hrj8-hjv8-mgwc • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/002/
ATT&CKT1059.002 T1190
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer testing legitimate multi-application AppleScript automations that nest tell blocks and intentionally use `do shell script` (e.g., DevOps scripts coordinating Chrome + Terminal)
  • Keyboard Maestro / BetterTouchTool / Alfred workflows that build complex AppleScripts on the fly and shell out for unrelated reasons
  • Anyquery legitimate use where the user manually passes a URL containing the literal substring `do shell script` in a benign context (extremely rare)
  • Security tooling / red-team automation that intentionally chains AppleScript and shell commands via `osascript -e`
Suppression filter
and not (InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("Keyboard Maestro Engine", "BetterTouchTool", "Alfred 5", "Hammerspoon") or InitiatingProcessFolderPath has_any ("/Applications/Keyboard Maestro", "/Applications/BetterTouchTool", "/Applications/Alfred"))
Response runbook
1) Triage — pull full ProcessCommandLine; confirm at least two `tell application` openers separated by an `end tell` + `do shell script` block (the breakout signature). Identify the parent: if it's `anyquery` or a process whose command line references `anyquery`, escalate. Capture the SQL/HTTP traffic that triggered the INSERT/UPDATE on chrome_tabs. 2) Contain — kill the osascript and parent anyquery processes; isolate the host via EDR; disable the anyquery HTTP listener (any local user reaching the endpoint can re-trigger). Snapshot ~/Library/Application Support and Keychain access logs. 3) Hunt — pivot on the user/host: list all child processes of anyquery in the last 30 days; check for `do shell script` patterns invoking curl/wget/python/bash; enumerate anyquery versions across the fleet and confirm patch level >= commit c651df0b8767; audit any HTTP endpoints exposing the anyquery SQL interface beyond localhost.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where DeviceInfo_OSPlatform == "macOS" or FolderPath has "/usr/bin/osascript"
| where FileName =~ "osascript"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "do shell script"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "end tell"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "tell application"
| extend TellOpeners = countof(tolower(ProcessCommandLine), "tell application")
| where TellOpeners >= 2
   or InitiatingProcessFileName has "anyquery"
   or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "anyquery"
   or InitiatingProcessFolderPath has "anyquery"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath,
          InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, SHA256, ProcessId
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ActingProcessName has "osascript"
| where ActingProcessCommandLine has "do shell script"
| where ActingProcessCommandLine has "end tell"
| where ActingProcessCommandLine has "tell application"
| extend TellOpeners = countof(tolower(ActingProcessCommandLine), "tell application")
| where TellOpeners >= 2
   or ParentProcessName has "anyquery"
   or ParentProcessCommandLine has "anyquery"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername,
          ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine,
          ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine,
          ActingProcessId, SHA256
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: AppleScript Code Injection via osascript Breakout Pattern (CVE-2026-47252 / Anyquery)
id: 8c4e3a91-2b7d-4f56-9e1a-3d5b8c7a6f12
description: Detects exploitation of CVE-2026-47252 where anyquery's chrome_tabs plugin interpolates a SQL-controlled URL into an AppleScript template, allowing a payload to close the tell block, inject 'do shell script', and reopen a new tell block.
references:
  - https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-hrj8-hjv8-mgwc
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/06/09
tags:
  - attack.execution
  - attack.t1059.002
  - attack.initial_access
  - attack.t1190
logsource:
  category: process_creation
  product: macos
detection:
  selection_osascript:
    Image|endswith: '/osascript'
  selection_injection:
    CommandLine|contains|all:
      - 'do shell script'
      - 'end tell'
      - 'tell application'
  selection_parent:
    ParentImage|contains: 'anyquery'
  condition: selection_osascript and selection_injection and selection_parent
level: high
@process.executable.path:*/osascript @process.command_line:(*"do shell script"* AND *"end tell"* AND *"tell application"*) AND (@process.parent.executable.path:*anyquery* OR @process.parent.command_line:*anyquery*)
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2
| ImageFileName=/\/osascript$/i
| regex("do shell script", field=CommandLine)
| regex("end tell", field=CommandLine)
| regex("tell application", field=CommandLine)
| ParentBaseFileName=/anyquery/i OR ParentImageFileName=/anyquery/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ParentBaseFileName, ImageFileName, CommandLine], function=count())

When “Hi, This Is IT” Comes Through Microsoft Teams

Unit 42 (Palo Alto)
2026-06-08 10 use cases 2 techniques 6 kill-chain phases detected

Attackers are increasingly targeting collaboration platforms like Microsoft Teams. Learn the risks and key steps to strengthen your organization's security. The post When “Hi, This Is IT” Comes Through Microsoft Teams appeared first on Unit 42 .

ATT&CKT1190 T1566
Domainsommicrosoft.com
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix

Detected

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] External MS Teams chat invite from IT-impersonating unmanaged or federated tenant · [LLM] Activity involving ommicrosoft.com Cloaked-Ursa Teams typosquat · Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender · Microsoft Teams external-tenant chat from unverified IT-helpdesk impersonator
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] MFA approval within minutes of inbound external Microsoft Teams chat · Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha)
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → RMM tool installed by non-IT user — remote-access utility for hands-on-keyboard
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
External MS Teams chat invite from IT-impersonating unmanaged or federated tenant AIDelivery Medium
Hunts for inbound Microsoft Teams chat invitations from external/guest users whose display name or sender domain impersonates internal IT/helpdesk/MSP functions — the exact technique Cloaked Ursa and UNC6692 used to initiate the social-engineering conversation before requesting MFA approval. Rationale: Maps to the article's central narrative — external Teams chat from an unmanaged/federated tenant whose sender display name impersonates IT/helpdesk/MSP. Combines the external-user predicate (#EXT#/onmicrosoft.com/UserType=Guest) with the IT-impersonation lexicon Unit 42 calls out as the lowered-suspicion lure.
ATT&CKT1566.003 T1656
Data sourcesAuthentication Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate inbound chat from a contracted MSP whose display name genuinely includes 'IT Support'
  • Vendor partners pinged via cross-tenant federation for break/fix conversations
  • Internal IT delegate accounts that are mailbox-shared with external collaborators
  • Sales reps from security/IT vendors initiating prospecting chats
  • Conference / training organizers whose tenant names contain 'IT'
Suppression filter
| where UserId !endswith "@your-trusted-msp.com" and UserId !endswith "@your-contracted-vendor.com"
Response runbook
1) Triage: pivot on UserId (sender), ClientIP, and UserAgent — check whether the sender tenant is on your federation Allow list and whether the sender domain typosquats your IT/MSP brand. Look up the recipient's recent chat/MFA history. 2) Contain: if confirmed external impersonation, remove the recipient's MFA-pending session, force token revocation (`Revoke-MgUserSignInSession`), and add the sender tenant or domain to the Teams external-domain Block list; if widespread, restrict Teams federation to allow-list mode (Figure 3 of the article). 3) Hunt: pivot on sender IP to find other recipients hit by the same external tenant; check SigninLogs for MFA prompts to those recipients in the 30 min window after each chat; check CloudAppEvents for any file/URL shared in the chat body.
CloudAppEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where Application =~ "Microsoft Teams"
| where ActionType in~ ("ChatCreated","MessageSent","MemberAdded","ChatRetrieved","MeetingDetailMessaged")
| extend RawStr = tostring(RawEventData), ActObjStr = tostring(ActivityObjects)
| where AccountType in~ ("Guest","External") or RawStr has_any ("#EXT#","onmicrosoft.com")
| where ActObjStr has_any ("IT Support","IT Department","Help Desk","Helpdesk","Service Desk","IT Admin","Security Team","SOC Team","Managed Service","MSP")
| project Timestamp, ActionType, AccountDisplayName, AccountId, AccountType, IPAddress, CountryCode, UserAgent, ObjectName, ActivityObjects, RawEventData
| order by Timestamp desc
OfficeActivity
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where OfficeWorkload =~ "MicrosoftTeams"
| where Operation in~ ("ChatCreated","MessageSent","MemberAdded","MemberRoleChanged")
| extend ParamsStr = tostring(Parameters)
| where UserType in ("Guest","External") or UserId has_any ("#EXT#","#ext#","onmicrosoft.com")
| where ParamsStr has_any ("IT Support","IT Department","Help Desk","Helpdesk","Service Desk","IT Admin","Security Team","SOC Team","Managed Service","MSP")
| project TimeGenerated, Operation, UserId, UserType, ClientIP, UserAgent, OfficeObjectId, ParamsStr
| order by TimeGenerated desc
source:(office365 OR azure.activeDirectory) (@Workload:MicrosoftTeams OR @evt.name:("ChatCreated" OR "MessageSent" OR "MemberAdded")) (@UserType:(Guest OR External) OR @UserId:(*#EXT#* OR *onmicrosoft.com*)) ("IT Support" OR "IT Department" OR "Help Desk" OR Helpdesk OR "Service Desk" OR "Security Team" OR "Managed Service" OR MSP)
APPLICATION
`cim_Authentication_indexes` sourcetype IN ("o365:management:activity","ms:o365:management") Workload="MicrosoftTeams" Operation IN ("ChatCreated","MessageSent","MemberAdded","MemberRoleChanged") 
| spath input=Parameters 
| eval is_external=if(match(UserId,"(?i)#EXT#|onmicrosoft\.com") OR UserType IN ("Guest","External"),1,0) 
| where is_external=1 
| eval impersonation=if(match(_raw,"(?i)(IT\s?Support|Help\s?Desk|Helpdesk|Service\s?Desk|IT\s?Department|IT\s?Admin|MSP|Managed\s?Service|Security\s?Team|SOC\s?Team)"),1,0) 
| where impersonation=1 
| table _time UserId UserType ClientIP UserAgent Operation OfficeWorkload Parameters 
| sort - _time
MFA approval within minutes of inbound external Microsoft Teams chat AIExploitation High
Correlates an inbound external Teams chat to a user with a successful MFA-satisfied sign-in by that same user inside a 30-minute window. Surfaces the Cloaked Ursa / UNC6692 pattern where the 'IT technician' chat is immediately followed by a victim-approved MFA push. Rationale: 30-minute window pairing an inbound external Teams chat with an MFA-satisfied sign-in by the same recipient maps directly to the article's narrative — 'IT technician' chat → 'approve this MFA' → compromise. Time-window correlation is the noise-killer the BluRaven kb prescribes for this scenario.
ATT&CKT1621 T1566.003
Data sourcesAuthentication
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate MSP help desk genuinely fixing a sign-in issue with a real user
  • Internal IT walking a user through SSO enrollment via a federated tenant
  • Vendor support chats where a user simultaneously re-authenticates to the same vendor portal
  • Conference Wi-Fi MFA prompt happening to fire during an unrelated external chat
  • Newly-onboarded user receiving both a welcome chat from an external HR consultant AND completing initial MFA setup
Suppression filter
| where SenderUpn !endswith "@your-trusted-msp.com"
Response runbook
1) Triage: open the chat sender's UserId — verify whether the sender tenant is on the Teams federation Allow list. Pull the SigninLogs MFA detail (was it push, SMS, OAuth code, FIDO2?) and the destination IP / country. 2) Contain: if the IP geo, ASN, or device fingerprint of the MFA-approved sign-in doesn't match the victim's baseline, revoke sessions immediately (`Revoke-MgUserSignInSession`), force credential reset, and block the sender tenant in Teams external-access settings. 3) Hunt: pivot on the MFA-success IP for the next 24 hours across SigninLogs — likely the same IP is harvesting other accounts the attacker chatted; also check OfficeActivity MailItemsAccessed, InboxRuleCreated, and CloudAppEvents OAuth grants under the compromised UPN.
let WindowMinutes = 30m;
let ExternalChats = CloudAppEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
    | where Application =~ "Microsoft Teams"
    | where ActionType in~ ("ChatCreated","MessageSent","MemberAdded")
    | extend RawStr = tostring(RawEventData)
    | where AccountType in~ ("Guest","External") or RawStr has_any ("#EXT#","onmicrosoft.com")
    | extend Recipient = tostring(parse_json(tostring(ActivityObjects))[0].Name)
    | project ChatTime = Timestamp, SenderUpn = AccountDisplayName, SenderIP = IPAddress, Recipient, ActionType;
AADSignInEventsBeta
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ErrorCode == 0
| where AuthenticationRequirement =~ "multiFactorAuthentication"
| extend AuthDetailStr = tostring(AuthenticationDetails)
| where AuthDetailStr has_any ("Mobile app notification","PhoneAppNotification","OAuth Verify","Authenticator App")
| join kind=inner ExternalChats on $left.AccountUpn == $right.Recipient
| where Timestamp between (ChatTime .. ChatTime + WindowMinutes)
| extend DelayMinutes = datetime_diff('minute', Timestamp, ChatTime)
| project ChatTime, MfaApprovalTime = Timestamp, DelayMinutes, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Country, City, Application, RiskLevelDuringSignIn, ConditionalAccessStatus, SenderUpn, SenderIP
| order by MfaApprovalTime desc
let WindowMinutes = 30m;
let ExternalChats = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
    | where OfficeWorkload =~ "MicrosoftTeams"
    | where Operation in~ ("ChatCreated","MessageSent","MemberAdded")
    | where UserType in ("Guest","External") or UserId has_any ("#EXT#","#ext#","onmicrosoft.com")
    | extend ParamsStr = tostring(Parameters)
    | extend Recipient = extract(@"(?i)([A-Za-z0-9._%+-]+@[A-Za-z0-9.-]+\.[A-Za-z]{2,})", 1, ParamsStr)
    | project ChatTime = TimeGenerated, SenderUpn = UserId, SenderIP = ClientIP, Recipient;
SigninLogs
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ResultType == 0
| where AuthenticationRequirement =~ "multiFactorAuthentication"
| extend AuthMethods = tostring(AuthenticationMethodsUsed)
| where AuthMethods has_any ("Mobile app notification","PhoneAppNotification","Microsoft Authenticator")
| join kind=inner ExternalChats on $left.UserPrincipalName == $right.Recipient
| where TimeGenerated between (ChatTime .. ChatTime + WindowMinutes)
| extend DelayMinutes = datetime_diff('minute', TimeGenerated, ChatTime)
| project ChatTime, MfaApprovalTime = TimeGenerated, DelayMinutes, UserPrincipalName, IPAddress, Location, AppDisplayName, RiskLevelDuringSignIn, ConditionalAccessStatus, SenderUpn, SenderIP
| order by MfaApprovalTime desc
source:azure.activeDirectory @evt.name:"Sign-in activity" @properties.status.errorCode:0 @properties.authenticationRequirement:multiFactorAuthentication (@properties.authenticationMethodsUsed:("Mobile app notification" OR "PhoneAppNotification" OR "Microsoft Authenticator"))
#event_simpleName=IdpAuthenticationLog
| Application=/microsoft|teams|office/i
| AuthenticationRequirement=/multiFactor/i
| Status=/success/i
| AuthenticationMethod=/(Mobile app notification|PhoneAppNotification|Microsoft Authenticator)/i
| groupBy([UserPrincipalName, IPAddress], function=count())
CLOUD

Activity involving ommicrosoft.com Cloaked-Ursa Teams typosquat AIDelivery High
Direct IOC hunt for the ommicrosoft.com typosquat domain referenced by Unit 42 in connection with Cloaked Ursa / Midnight Blizzard Teams credential-harvesting infrastructure. Sweeps Teams/Office activity, AAD sign-ins, DNS resolutions, and proxy traffic for any reference to the domain. Rationale: ommicrosoft.com is the only hard IOC in the article — a single-character typosquat of microsoft.com that Unit 42 ties to Cloaked Ursa Teams credential-harvesting. Any resolution, web request, sign-in reference, or Teams artifact mentioning this string is high-fidelity. Union covers chat, sign-in, DNS, and proxy surfaces because the same domain may surface across all four during the kill chain.
ATT&CKT1583.001 T1566.003 T1656
Data sourcesNetwork_Resolution Web Authentication
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Threat-intel platform / TIP fetching the IOC for analysis
  • Security researcher manually pivoting on the domain
  • Sandbox detonation submitting URL for analysis
  • Phishing-simulation vendor staging a lookalike for an awareness exercise
  • Mail security gateway sandbox following the link from a quarantined message
Suppression filter
| where SrcIP !in ("<SOC sandbox IP>","<TIP enrichment IP>")
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the source — internal SOC tooling vs end-user host. If a Teams sender UPN or sign-in UPN contains the domain, treat as confirmed Cloaked Ursa attempted contact. 2) Contain: sinkhole / block ommicrosoft.com at the proxy and resolver tier, block the sender tenant in Teams external access, and (for any user with a sign-in attempt or click) revoke sessions and force credential rotation. Push an Entra ID Conditional Access policy blocking sign-ins where Resource or App matches the IOC. 3) Hunt: pivot on the resolved IP(s) over the last 90 days in DnsEvents / DeviceNetworkEvents and proxy logs; check UrlClickEvents for any click-through; review OfficeActivity for chat history from the sender tenant to enumerate additional victims.
let IOC = dynamic(["ommicrosoft.com"]);
union isfuzzy=true
    (DeviceNetworkEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
        | where RemoteUrl has_any (IOC) or AdditionalFields has_any (IOC)
        | project Timestamp, Source="NetworkEvents", DeviceName, AccountUpn=InitiatingProcessAccountUpn, InitiatingProcessFileName, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP),
    (UrlClickEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
        | where Url has_any (IOC)
        | project Timestamp, Source="UrlClickEvents", DeviceName="", AccountUpn, InitiatingProcessFileName="", RemoteUrl=Url, RemoteIP=tostring(IPAddress)),
    (CloudAppEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
        | where Application =~ "Microsoft Teams"
        | extend Raw = tostring(RawEventData), Acts = tostring(ActivityObjects)
        | where Raw has_any (IOC) or Acts has_any (IOC)
        | project Timestamp, Source="Teams", DeviceName="", AccountUpn=AccountDisplayName, InitiatingProcessFileName=ActionType, RemoteUrl=ObjectName, RemoteIP=IPAddress),
    (AADSignInEventsBeta
        | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
        | where AccountUpn has_any (IOC) or AlternateSignInName has_any (IOC) or ResourceDisplayName has_any (IOC)
        | project Timestamp, Source="AADSignIn", DeviceName=DeviceName, AccountUpn, InitiatingProcessFileName=Application, RemoteUrl=ResourceDisplayName, RemoteIP=IPAddress)
| order by Timestamp desc
let IOC = dynamic(["ommicrosoft.com"]);
union isfuzzy=true
    (OfficeActivity
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
        | where UserId has_any (IOC) or tostring(Parameters) has_any (IOC)
        | project TimeGenerated, Source="OfficeActivity", Operation, User=UserId, SrcIP=ClientIP, Detail=tostring(Parameters)),
    (SigninLogs
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
        | where UserPrincipalName has_any (IOC) or AppDisplayName has_any (IOC) or ResourceDisplayName has_any (IOC) or AlternateSignInName has_any (IOC)
        | project TimeGenerated, Source="SigninLogs", Operation=OperationName, User=UserPrincipalName, SrcIP=IPAddress, Detail=strcat(AppDisplayName," | ",ResultDescription)),
    (DnsEvents
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
        | where Name has_any (IOC)
        | project TimeGenerated, Source="DnsEvents", Operation=QueryTypeName, User="", SrcIP=ClientIP, Detail=Name),
    (CommonSecurityLog
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
        | where DestinationHostName has_any (IOC) or RequestURL has_any (IOC) or DestinationDnsDomain has_any (IOC)
        | project TimeGenerated, Source="Proxy/CSL", Operation=DeviceAction, User=SourceUserName, SrcIP=SourceIP, Detail=coalesce(RequestURL, DestinationHostName))
| order by TimeGenerated desc
